<li value="1"><a href="/wiki/Pristina" title="Pristina">Pristina</a> is the official capital.<sup id="cite_ref-capital_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-capital-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Prizren" title="Prizren">Prizren</a> is the historic capital of Kosovo.<sup id="cite_ref-capital_10-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-capital-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup></li><li value="2">The Euro is the official currency in Kosovo even though Kosovo is not a formal member of the <a href="/wiki/Eurozone" title="Eurozone">eurozone</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Serbian_Dinar" class="mw-redirect" title="Serbian Dinar">Serbian Dinar</a> is sometimes accepted in Serb-majority areas.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup></li><li value="3">XK is a "user assigned" ISO 3166 code not designated by the standard, but used by the <a href="/wiki/European_Commission" title="European Commission">European Commission</a>, Switzerland, the <a href="/wiki/Deutsche_Bundesbank" title="Deutsche Bundesbank">Deutsche Bundesbank</a> and other organisations. However, <a href="/wiki/ISO_3166-2:RS" title="ISO 3166-2:RS">ISO 3166-2:RS-KM</a> remains in use.</li>
</ol></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Kosovo</b> (<a href="/wiki/Albanian_language" title="Albanian language">Albanian</a>: <i lang="sq">Kosova</i> <small></small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="sq-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Albanian" title="Help:IPA/Albanian">[kɔˈsɔva]</a></span>; <a href="/wiki/Serbian_Cyrillic_alphabet" title="Serbian Cyrillic alphabet">Serbian Cyrillic</a>: <span lang="sr-Cyrl">Косово</span> <small></small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="und-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Serbo-Croatian" title="Help:IPA/Serbo-Croatian">[kôsoʋo]</a></span>), officially the <b>Republic of Kosovo</b> (Albanian: <i lang="sq">Republika e Kosovës</i>; Serbian: <span lang="sr">Република Косово</span>, <small>romanized:&#160;</small><span title="Serbian-language romanization"><i lang="sr-Latn">Republika Kosovo</i></span>), is a country in <a href="/wiki/Southeast_Europe" title="Southeast Europe">Southeast Europe</a> with <a href="/wiki/International_recognition_of_Kosovo" title="International recognition of Kosovo">partial diplomatic recognition</a>.  Kosovo lies landlocked in the centre of the <a href="/wiki/Balkans" title="Balkans">Balkans</a>, bordered by <a href="/wiki/Serbia" title="Serbia">Serbia</a> to the north and east, <a href="/wiki/North_Macedonia" title="North Macedonia">North Macedonia</a> to the southeast, <a href="/wiki/Albania" title="Albania">Albania</a> to the southwest, and <a href="/wiki/Montenegro" title="Montenegro">Montenegro</a> to the west. Most of central Kosovo is dominated by the vast plains and fields of <a href="/wiki/Metohija" title="Metohija">Metohija</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Kosovo_field" title="Kosovo field">Kosovo field</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Accursed_Mountains" title="Accursed Mountains">Accursed Mountains</a> and <a href="/wiki/%C5%A0ar_Mountains" title="Šar Mountains">Šar Mountains</a> rise in the southwest and southeast, respectively. Its capital and largest city is <a href="/wiki/Pristina" title="Pristina">Pristina</a>.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Dardani" title="Dardani">Dardani</a> tribe emerged in Kosovo and established the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Dardania" title="Kingdom of Dardania">Kingdom of Dardania</a> in the 4th century BC. It was later annexed by the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Empire" title="Roman Empire">Roman Empire</a> in the 1st century BC. The territory remained in the <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_Empire" title="Byzantine Empire">Byzantine Empire</a>, facing Slavic invasions from the 6th-7th century AD. Control shifted between the Byzantines and the <a href="/wiki/First_Bulgarian_Empire" title="First Bulgarian Empire">First Bulgarian Empire</a>. In the 13th century, Kosovo became integral to the <a href="/wiki/Serbia_in_the_Middle_Ages" title="Serbia in the Middle Ages">Serbian medieval state</a> and the seat of the <a href="/wiki/Serbian_Orthodox_Church" title="Serbian Orthodox Church">Serbian Orthodox Church</a>. <a href="/wiki/Ottoman_wars_in_Europe" title="Ottoman wars in Europe">Ottoman expansion</a> in the Balkans in the late 14th and 15th century led to the decline and <a href="/wiki/Fall_of_the_Serbian_Empire" title="Fall of the Serbian Empire">fall of the Serbian Empire</a>; the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Kosovo" title="Battle of Kosovo">Battle of Kosovo</a> of 1389 is considered to be one of the defining moments. The <a href="/wiki/Ottoman_Empire" title="Ottoman Empire">Ottoman Empire</a> fully conquered Kosovo after the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Kosovo_(1448)" title="Battle of Kosovo (1448)">Second Battle of Kosovo</a>, ruling for nearly five centuries until 1912. Kosovo was the center of the <a href="/wiki/Albanian_National_Awakening" title="Albanian National Awakening">Albanian Renaissance</a> and experienced the <a href="/wiki/Albanian_revolt_of_1910" title="Albanian revolt of 1910">Albanian revolts of 1910</a> and <a href="/wiki/Albanian_revolt_of_1912" title="Albanian revolt of 1912">1912</a>. After the <a href="/wiki/Balkan_Wars" title="Balkan Wars">Balkan Wars</a>, it was ceded to Serbia and Montenegro and became an <a href="/wiki/Socialist_Autonomous_Province_of_Kosovo" title="Socialist Autonomous Province of Kosovo">Autonomous Province</a> within Yugoslavia. Tensions between Kosovo's Albanian and Serb communities simmered through the 20th century and occasionally erupted into major violence, culminating in the <a href="/wiki/Kosovo_War" title="Kosovo War">Kosovo War</a> of 1998 and 1999, which resulted in the withdrawal of the Yugoslav army and the establishment of the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations_Interim_Administration_Mission_in_Kosovo" title="United Nations Interim Administration Mission in Kosovo">United Nations Interim Administration Mission in Kosovo</a>.
</p><p>Kosovo <a href="/wiki/Unilateral_declaration_of_independence" title="Unilateral declaration of independence">unilaterally</a>  <a href="/wiki/2008_Kosovo_declaration_of_independence" title="2008 Kosovo declaration of independence">declared its independence</a> from <a href="/wiki/Serbia" title="Serbia">Serbia</a> on 17 February 2008,<sup id="cite_ref-icj2020_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-icj2020-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> and has since gained diplomatic recognition as a <a href="/wiki/Sovereign_state" title="Sovereign state">sovereign state</a> by <a href="/wiki/International_recognition_of_Kosovo" title="International recognition of Kosovo">101 member states</a> of the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations" title="United Nations">United Nations</a>. Although Serbia does not officially recognise Kosovo as a sovereign state and continues to claim it as its constituent <a href="/wiki/Autonomous_Province_of_Kosovo_and_Metohija" title="Autonomous Province of Kosovo and Metohija">Autonomous Province of Kosovo and Metohija</a>, it accepts the governing authority of the Kosovo institutions as a part of the <a href="/wiki/Brussels_Agreement_(2013)" title="Brussels Agreement (2013)">2013 Brussels Agreement</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-foreignaffairs_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-foreignaffairs-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Kosovo is a developing country, with an <a href="/wiki/Economy_of_Kosovo" title="Economy of Kosovo">upper-middle-income economy</a>. It has experienced solid <a href="/wiki/International_rankings_of_Kosovo" title="International rankings of Kosovo">economic growth</a> over the last decade as measured by international financial institutions since the onset of the <a href="/wiki/Financial_crisis_of_2007%E2%80%932008" class="mw-redirect" title="Financial crisis of 2007–2008">financial crisis of 2007–2008</a>. Kosovo is a member of the <a href="/wiki/International_Monetary_Fund" title="International Monetary Fund">International Monetary Fund</a>, <a href="/wiki/World_Bank" title="World Bank">World Bank</a>, <a href="/wiki/European_Bank_for_Reconstruction_and_Development" title="European Bank for Reconstruction and Development">EBRD</a>, <a href="/wiki/Venice_Commission" title="Venice Commission">Venice Commission</a>, the <a href="/wiki/International_Olympic_Committee" title="International Olympic Committee">International Olympic Committee</a>, and has applied for membership in the <a href="/wiki/Council_of_Europe" title="Council of Europe">Council of Europe</a>, <a href="/wiki/UNESCO" title="UNESCO">UNESCO</a>, <a href="/wiki/Interpol" title="Interpol">Interpol</a>, and for observer status in the <a href="/wiki/Organisation_of_Islamic_Cooperation" title="Organisation of Islamic Cooperation">Organisation of Islamic Cooperation</a>. In December 2022, Kosovo filed <a href="/wiki/Accession_of_Kosovo_to_the_European_Union" title="Accession of Kosovo to the European Union">a formal application</a> to become a member of the <a href="/wiki/European_Union" title="European Union">European Union</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-dw10_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dw10-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Name">Name</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Names_of_Kosovo" title="Names of Kosovo">Names of Kosovo</a></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Etymology">Etymology</span></h3>
<p>The name <i>Kosovo</i> is of South Slavic origin. Kosovo (<a href="/wiki/Serbian_Cyrillic_alphabet" title="Serbian Cyrillic alphabet">Serbian Cyrillic</a>: <span lang="sr-Cyrl">Косово</span>, <small>Serbian pronunciation:&#160;</small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="und-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Serbo-Croatian" title="Help:IPA/Serbo-Croatian">[kôsoʋo]</a></span>) is the Serbian neuter possessive adjective of <i>kos</i> (кос) "<a href="/wiki/Common_blackbird" title="Common blackbird">blackbird</a>",<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> an <a href="/wiki/Ellipsis_(linguistics)" title="Ellipsis (linguistics)">ellipsis</a> for <i>Kosovo Polje</i>, 'blackbird field', the name of <a href="/wiki/Kosovo_field_(region)" class="mw-redirect" title="Kosovo field (region)">a karst field</a> situated in the eastern half of today's Kosovo and the site of the 1389 <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Kosovo" title="Battle of Kosovo">Battle of Kosovo Field</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Everett-Heath2000_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Everett-Heath2000-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> The name of the karst field was for the first time applied to a wider area when the <a href="/wiki/Kosovo_vilayet" title="Kosovo vilayet">Ottoman Vilayet of Kosovo</a> was created in 1877.
</p><p>The entire territory that corresponds to today's country is commonly referred to in English simply as <i>Kosovo</i> and in <a href="/wiki/Albanian_language" title="Albanian language">Albanian</a> as <i>Kosova</i> (<a href="/wiki/Albanian_grammar" class="mw-redirect" title="Albanian grammar">definite form</a>, <small>Albanian pronunciation:&#160;</small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="sq-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Albanian" title="Help:IPA/Albanian">[kɔˈsɔva]</a></span>) or <i><span title="Albanian-language text"><i lang="sq">Kosovë</i></span></i> ("indefinite" form, <small>Albanian pronunciation:&#160;</small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="sq-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Albanian" title="Help:IPA/Albanian">[kɔˈsɔvə]</a></span>). In Serbia, a formal distinction is made between the eastern and western areas of the country; the term <i><span title="Serbian-language text"><i lang="sr-Latn">Kosovo</i></span></i> (<span title="Serbian-language text"><span lang="sr">Косово</span></span>) is used for the eastern part of Kosovo centred on the historical <a href="/wiki/Kosovo_field_(region)" class="mw-redirect" title="Kosovo field (region)">Kosovo Field</a>, while the western part of the territory of Kosovo is called <i><a href="/wiki/Metohija" title="Metohija">Metohija</a></i> (<a href="/wiki/Albanian_language" title="Albanian language">Albanian</a>: <i lang="sq">Dukagjin</i>). Thus, in Serbia the entire Kosovo is called by the dual name <i>Kosovo and Metohija</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-constitution-serbia_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-constitution-serbia-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Modern_usuage">Modern usuage</span></h3>
<p>Some Albanians also prefer to refer to Kosovo as <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Dardania" title="Kingdom of Dardania"><i>Dardania</i></a>, the name of an ancient kingdom and later <a href="/wiki/Dardania_(Roman_province)" title="Dardania (Roman province)">Roman province</a>, which covered the territory of modern-day Kosovo. The name is dervied from the ancient tribe of the <i><a href="/wiki/Dardani" title="Dardani">Dardani</a></i>, which is considered be related to the Proto-Albanian term <i>dardā</i>, which means "pear" (Modern Albanian: <span title="Albanian-language text"><i lang="sq">dardhë</i></span>).<sup id="cite_ref-Everett-Heath2000_19-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Everett-Heath2000-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> The former Kosovo President <a href="/wiki/Ibrahim_Rugova" title="Ibrahim Rugova">Ibrahim Rugova</a> had been an enthusiastic backer of a "Dardanian" identity, and the Kosovar presidential flag and seal refer to this national identity. However, the name "Kosova" remains more widely used among the Albanian population. The flag of Dardania remains in use as the official <a href="/wiki/President_of_Kosovo" title="President of Kosovo">Presidential seal and standard</a> and is heavily featured in the institution of the presidency of the country.
</p><p>The official conventional long name of the state is <i>Republic of Kosovo</i>, as defined by the <a href="/wiki/Constitution_of_Kosovo" title="Constitution of Kosovo">Constitution of Kosovo</a>, and is used to represent Kosovo internationally.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> Additionally, as a result of an <a href="/wiki/Belgrade%E2%80%93Pristina_negotiations" title="Belgrade–Pristina negotiations">arrangement agreed between Pristina and Belgrade</a> in talks mediated by the European Union, Kosovo has participated in some international forums and organizations under the title "Kosovo*" with a footnote stating, "This designation is without prejudice to positions on status, and is in line with UNSC 1244 and the ICJ Opinion on the Kosovo declaration of independence". This arrangement, which has been dubbed the "asterisk agreement", was agreed in an 11-point arrangement on 24 February 2012.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="History">History</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">For a chronological guide, see <a href="/wiki/Timeline_of_Kosovo_history" title="Timeline of Kosovo history">Timeline of Kosovo history</a>.</div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/History_of_Kosovo" title="History of Kosovo">History of Kosovo</a></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Antiquity">Antiquity</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Archaeology_of_Kosovo" title="Archaeology of Kosovo">Archaeology of Kosovo</a> and <a href="/wiki/Copper,_Bronze_and_Iron_Age_sites_in_Kosovo" title="Copper, Bronze and Iron Age sites in Kosovo">Copper, Bronze and Iron Age sites in Kosovo</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Illyrians" title="Illyrians">Illyrians</a> and <a href="/wiki/Dardania_(Roman_province)" title="Dardania (Roman province)">Dardania</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Hyjnesha_muze.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d6/Hyjnesha_muze.jpg/220px-Hyjnesha_muze.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d6/Hyjnesha_muze.jpg/330px-Hyjnesha_muze.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d6/Hyjnesha_muze.jpg/440px-Hyjnesha_muze.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4288" data-file-height="2848" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Goddess_on_the_Throne" title="Goddess on the Throne">Goddess on the Throne</a> is one of the most significant archaeological artifacts of Kosovo and has been adopted as the symbol of <a href="/wiki/Pristina" title="Pristina">Pristina</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The strategic position including the abundant natural resources were favorable for the development of human settlements in Kosovo, as is highlighted by the hundreds of archaeological sites identified throughout its territory. The first archaeological expedition in Kosovo was organized by the Austro-Hungarian army during the <a href="/wiki/World_War_I" title="World War I">World War I</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Illyrians" title="Illyrians">Illyrian</a> <a href="/wiki/Tumuli" class="mw-redirect" title="Tumuli">tumuli</a> burial grounds of Nepërbishti within the <a href="/wiki/District_of_Prizren" title="District of Prizren">district of Prizren</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-SchermerShukriu_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SchermerShukriu-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> Since 2000, the increase in archaeological expeditions has revealed many, previously unknown sites. The earliest documented traces in Kosovo are associated to the <a href="/wiki/Stone_Age" title="Stone Age">Stone Age</a>; namely, indications that cave dwellings might have existed, such as Radivojce Cave near the source of the <a href="/wiki/Drin_(river)" title="Drin (river)">Drin River</a>, Grnčar Cave in <a href="/wiki/Viti,_Kosovo" title="Viti, Kosovo">Viti municipality</a> and the Dema and Karamakaz Caves in the <a href="/wiki/Peja" title="Peja">municipality of Peja</a>.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Dardanian_Kingdom_(late_3rd_century_BC).png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/24/Dardanian_Kingdom_%28late_3rd_century_BC%29.png/220px-Dardanian_Kingdom_%28late_3rd_century_BC%29.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="213" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/24/Dardanian_Kingdom_%28late_3rd_century_BC%29.png/330px-Dardanian_Kingdom_%28late_3rd_century_BC%29.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/24/Dardanian_Kingdom_%28late_3rd_century_BC%29.png/440px-Dardanian_Kingdom_%28late_3rd_century_BC%29.png 2x" data-file-width="3190" data-file-height="3086" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Dardania" title="Kingdom of Dardania">Kingdom of Dardania</a> in the 3rd century BCE.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The earliest archaeological evidence of organized settlement, which have been found in Kosovo, belong to the <a href="/wiki/Neolithic" title="Neolithic">Neolithic</a> <a href="/wiki/Star%C4%8Devo_culture" title="Starčevo culture">Starčevo</a> and <a href="/wiki/Vin%C4%8Da_culture" title="Vinča culture">Vinča</a> cultures.<sup id="cite_ref-Berisha_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Berisha-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Vlashnj%C3%AB" title="Vlashnjë">Vlashnjë</a> and <a href="/wiki/Runik" title="Runik">Runik</a> are important sites of the <a href="/wiki/Neolithic_sites_in_Kosovo" title="Neolithic sites in Kosovo">Neolithic era</a> with the rock art paintings at Mrrizi i Kobajës near <a href="/wiki/Vlashnj%C3%AB" title="Vlashnjë">Vlashnjë</a> being the first find of prehistoric art in Kosovo.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> Amongst the finds of excavations in Neolithic Runik is a baked-clay <a href="/wiki/Ocarina" title="Ocarina">ocarina</a>, which is the first musical instrument recorded in Kosovo.<sup id="cite_ref-Berisha_25-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Berisha-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> The beginning of the <a href="/wiki/Bronze_Age_Europe" title="Bronze Age Europe">Bronze Age</a> coincides with the presence of <a href="/wiki/Tumuli" class="mw-redirect" title="Tumuli">tumuli</a> burial grounds in western Kosovo, like the site of <a href="/wiki/Romaj%C3%AB" title="Romajë">Romajë</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-SchermerShukriu_24-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SchermerShukriu-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Dardani" title="Dardani">Dardani</a> were the most important <a href="/wiki/Paleo-Balkan_languages" title="Paleo-Balkan languages">Paleo-Balkan</a> tribe in the region of Kosovo. A wide area which consists of Kosovo, parts of Northern Macedonia and eastern Serbia was named <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Dardania" title="Kingdom of Dardania">Dardania</a> after them in classical antiquity, reaching to the <a href="/wiki/Thraco-Illyrian" title="Thraco-Illyrian">Thraco-Illyrian</a> contact zone in the east. In archaeological research, Illyrian names are predominant in western Dardania, while Thracian names are mostly found in eastern Dardania.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:ULPIANA_foto_Arben_Llapashtica_2016.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3c/ULPIANA_foto_Arben_Llapashtica_2016.jpg/220px-ULPIANA_foto_Arben_Llapashtica_2016.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="126" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3c/ULPIANA_foto_Arben_Llapashtica_2016.jpg/330px-ULPIANA_foto_Arben_Llapashtica_2016.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3c/ULPIANA_foto_Arben_Llapashtica_2016.jpg/440px-ULPIANA_foto_Arben_Llapashtica_2016.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4320" data-file-height="2467" /></a><figcaption>Ruins of Ancient <a href="/wiki/Ulpiana" title="Ulpiana">Ulpiana</a> situated southeast of <a href="/wiki/Pristina" title="Pristina">Pristina</a>. The city, built by <a href="/wiki/Trajan" title="Trajan">Trajan</a>, was an important political, cultural, and economic center of the Roman province of Dardania.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Thracian names are absent in western Dardania, while some Illyrian names appear in the eastern parts. Thus, their identification as either an <a href="/wiki/Illyrians" title="Illyrians">Illyrian</a> or <a href="/wiki/Thracians" title="Thracians">Thracian</a> tribe has been a subject of debate, the ethnolinguistic relationship between the two groups being largely uncertain and debated itself as well. The correspondence of Illyrian names, including those of the ruling elite, in Dardania with those of the southern Illyrians suggests a thracianization of parts of Dardania.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> The Dardani retained an individuality and continued to maintain social independence after Roman conquest, playing an important role in the formation of new groupings in the Roman era.<sup id="cite_ref-Papazoglou_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Papazoglou-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Roman state annexed Dardania by the first century AD. The importance of the area lay in its high mining potential, highlighted by the large mining complex of <a href="/wiki/Municipium_Dardanorum" title="Municipium Dardanorum">Municipium Dardanorum</a> and the designation of part of the region as an imperial mining district.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (March 2023)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Kosovo was part of two provinces, <a href="/wiki/Praevalitana" title="Praevalitana">Praevalitana</a> and <a href="/wiki/Dardania_(Roman_province)" title="Dardania (Roman province)">Dardania</a>. <a href="/wiki/Ulpiana" title="Ulpiana">Ulpiana</a> is the most important <a href="/wiki/Municipium" title="Municipium">municipium</a> which developed in Kosovo.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETeichner201581_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETeichner201581-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> It was refounded as Justiniana Secunda under <a href="/wiki/Justinian" class="mw-redirect" title="Justinian">Justinian</a> in the 6th century AD.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETeichner201583_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETeichner201583-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Middle_Ages">Middle Ages</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Gracanica_1.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/Gracanica_1.jpg/220px-Gracanica_1.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="145" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/Gracanica_1.jpg/330px-Gracanica_1.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/Gracanica_1.jpg/440px-Gracanica_1.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4280" data-file-height="2830" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Gra%C4%8Danica_Monastery" title="Gračanica Monastery">Gračanica Monastery</a>, a <a href="/wiki/World_Heritage_Site" title="World Heritage Site">UNESCO World Heritage Site</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Manastir_Visoki_De%C4%8Dani_(%D0%9C%D0%B0%D0%BD%D0%B0%D1%81%D1%82%D0%B8%D1%80_%D0%92%D0%B8%D1%81%D0%BE%D0%BA%D0%B8_%D0%94%D0%B5%D1%87%D0%B0%D0%BD%D0%B8)_-_by_Pudelek..jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0d/Manastir_Visoki_De%C4%8Dani_%28%D0%9C%D0%B0%D0%BD%D0%B0%D1%81%D1%82%D0%B8%D1%80_%D0%92%D0%B8%D1%81%D0%BE%D0%BA%D0%B8_%D0%94%D0%B5%D1%87%D0%B0%D0%BD%D0%B8%29_-_by_Pudelek..jpg/220px-Manastir_Visoki_De%C4%8Dani_%28%D0%9C%D0%B0%D0%BD%D0%B0%D1%81%D1%82%D0%B8%D1%80_%D0%92%D0%B8%D1%81%D0%BE%D0%BA%D0%B8_%D0%94%D0%B5%D1%87%D0%B0%D0%BD%D0%B8%29_-_by_Pudelek..jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="176" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0d/Manastir_Visoki_De%C4%8Dani_%28%D0%9C%D0%B0%D0%BD%D0%B0%D1%81%D1%82%D0%B8%D1%80_%D0%92%D0%B8%D1%81%D0%BE%D0%BA%D0%B8_%D0%94%D0%B5%D1%87%D0%B0%D0%BD%D0%B8%29_-_by_Pudelek..jpg/330px-Manastir_Visoki_De%C4%8Dani_%28%D0%9C%D0%B0%D0%BD%D0%B0%D1%81%D1%82%D0%B8%D1%80_%D0%92%D0%B8%D1%81%D0%BE%D0%BA%D0%B8_%D0%94%D0%B5%D1%87%D0%B0%D0%BD%D0%B8%29_-_by_Pudelek..jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0d/Manastir_Visoki_De%C4%8Dani_%28%D0%9C%D0%B0%D0%BD%D0%B0%D1%81%D1%82%D0%B8%D1%80_%D0%92%D0%B8%D1%81%D0%BE%D0%BA%D0%B8_%D0%94%D0%B5%D1%87%D0%B0%D0%BD%D0%B8%29_-_by_Pudelek..jpg/440px-Manastir_Visoki_De%C4%8Dani_%28%D0%9C%D0%B0%D0%BD%D0%B0%D1%81%D1%82%D0%B8%D1%80_%D0%92%D0%B8%D1%81%D0%BE%D0%BA%D0%B8_%D0%94%D0%B5%D1%87%D0%B0%D0%BD%D0%B8%29_-_by_Pudelek..jpg 2x" data-file-width="2337" data-file-height="1869" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Visoki_De%C4%8Dani_Monastery" class="mw-redirect" title="Visoki Dečani Monastery">Visoki Dečani Monastery</a>, a <a href="/wiki/World_Heritage_Site" title="World Heritage Site">UNESCO World Heritage Site</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>In the next centuries, Kosovo was a frontier province of the <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_Empire" title="Byzantine Empire">Byzantine Empire</a>. The region was exposed to an increasing number of raids from the 4th century CE onward, culminating with the <a href="/wiki/Slavic_migrations" class="mw-redirect" title="Slavic migrations">Slavic migrations</a> of the 6th and 7th centuries.
</p><p>Toponymic evidence suggests that <a href="/wiki/Albanian_language" title="Albanian language">Albanian</a> was probably spoken in Kosovo prior to the Slavic settlement of the region.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Prendergast2017_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Prendergast2017-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>There is one intriguing line of argument to suggest that the Slav presence in Kosovo and southernmost part of the Morava valley may have been quite weak in the first one or two centuries of Slav settlement. Only in the ninth century can the expansion of a strong Slav (or quasi-Slav) power into this region be observed. Under a series of ambitious rulers, the Bulgarians pushed westwards across modern Macedonia and eastern Serbia, until by the 850's they had taken over Kosovo and were pressing on the border of <a href="/wiki/Principality_of_Serbia_(early_medieval)" title="Principality of Serbia (early medieval)">Serbian Principality</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/First_Bulgarian_Empire" title="First Bulgarian Empire">First Bulgarian Empire</a> acquired Kosovo by the mid-9th century, but Byzantine control was <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_conquest_of_Bulgaria" title="Byzantine conquest of Bulgaria">restored</a> by the late 10th century. In 1072, the leaders of the Bulgarian <a href="/wiki/Uprising_of_Georgi_Voiteh" class="mw-redirect" title="Uprising of Georgi Voiteh">Uprising of Georgi Voiteh</a> traveled from their center in <a href="/wiki/Skopje" title="Skopje">Skopje</a> to Prizren and held a meeting in which they invited <a href="/wiki/Mihailo_Vojislavljevi%C4%87" class="mw-redirect" title="Mihailo Vojislavljević">Mihailo Vojislavljević</a> of <a href="/wiki/Duklja" title="Duklja">Duklja</a> to send them assistance. Mihailo sent his son, <a href="/wiki/Constantine_Bodin" title="Constantine Bodin">Constantine Bodin</a> with 300 of his soldiers. After they met, the Bulgarian magnates proclaimed him "Emperor of the Bulgarians".<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Demetrios_Chomatenos" title="Demetrios Chomatenos">Demetrios Chomatenos</a> is the last Byzantine archbishop of <a href="/wiki/Ohrid" title="Ohrid">Ohrid</a> to include Prizren in his jurisdiction until 1219.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Stefan_Nemanja" title="Stefan Nemanja">Stefan Nemanja</a> had seized the area along the <a href="/wiki/White_Drin" title="White Drin">White Drin</a> in 1185 to 1195 and the ecclesiastical split of Prizren from the Patriarchate in 1219 was the final act of establishing <a href="/wiki/Nemanji%C4%87_dynasty" title="Nemanjić dynasty">Nemanjić</a> rule. <a href="/wiki/Konstantin_Jire%C4%8Dek" title="Konstantin Jireček">Konstantin Jireček</a> concluded, from the correspondence of archbishop Demetrios of Ohrid from 1216 to 1236, that Dardania was increasingly populated by Albanians and the expansion started from <a href="/wiki/Gjakova" title="Gjakova">Gjakova</a> and <a href="/wiki/Prizren" title="Prizren">Prizren</a> area, prior to the Slavic expansion.<sup id="cite_ref-Abulafia1999_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Abulafia1999-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Balkans_1265.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5b/Balkans_1265.jpg/220px-Balkans_1265.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="155" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5b/Balkans_1265.jpg/330px-Balkans_1265.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5b/Balkans_1265.jpg/440px-Balkans_1265.jpg 2x" data-file-width="600" data-file-height="422" /></a><figcaption>Map of Southeastern Europe in 1265, including the <a href="/wiki/Medieval_Kingdom_of_Serbia" class="mw-redirect" title="Medieval Kingdom of Serbia">Medieval Kingdom of Serbia</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>During the 13th and 14th centuries, Kosovo was a political, cultural and religious centre of the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Serbia_(medieval)" title="Kingdom of Serbia (medieval)">Serbian Kingdom</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Sharpe_2003_364_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Sharpe_2003_364-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> In the late 13th century, the seat of the <a href="/wiki/Serbian_Orthodox_Church" title="Serbian Orthodox Church">Serbian Archbishopric</a> was moved to <a href="/wiki/Peja" title="Peja">Peja</a>, and rulers centred themselves between <a href="/wiki/Prizren" title="Prizren">Prizren</a> and <a href="/wiki/Skopje" title="Skopje">Skopje</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> during which time thousands of Christian monasteries and feudal-style forts and castles were erected,<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Stefan_Du%C5%A1an" title="Stefan Dušan">Stefan Dušan</a> using <a href="/wiki/Prizren_Fortress" title="Prizren Fortress">Prizren Fortress</a> as one of his temporary courts for a time. When the Serbian Empire fragmented into a conglomeration of principalities in 1371, Kosovo became the hereditary land of the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Brankovi%C4%87" class="mw-redirect" title="House of Branković">House of Branković</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Sharpe_2003_364_37-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Sharpe_2003_364-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> During the late 14th and early 15th centuries, parts of Kosovo, the easternmost area located near Pristina, were part of the <a href="/wiki/Principality_of_Dukagjini" title="Principality of Dukagjini">Principality of Dukagjini</a>, which was later incorporated into an anti-Ottoman federation of all Albanian principalities, the <a href="/wiki/League_of_Lezh%C3%AB" title="League of Lezhë">League of Lezhë</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Sellers2010_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Sellers2010-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Medieval_Monuments_in_Kosovo" title="Medieval Monuments in Kosovo">Medieval Monuments in Kosovo</a> is a combined <a href="/wiki/World_Heritage_Site" title="World Heritage Site">UNESCO World Heritage Site</a> consisting of four Serbian Orthodox churches and <a href="/wiki/List_of_Serbian_Orthodox_monasteries" title="List of Serbian Orthodox monasteries">monasteries</a>. The constructions were founded by members of the <a href="/wiki/Nemanji%C4%87_dynasty" title="Nemanjić dynasty">Nemanjić dynasty</a>, a prominent dynasty of <a href="/wiki/Serbia_in_the_Middle_Ages" title="Serbia in the Middle Ages">Middle Age Serbia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Ottoman_rule">Ottoman rule</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Bajrak_of_O%C5%A1trozub" title="Bajrak of Oštrozub">Bajrak of Oštrozub</a>, <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Kosovo" title="Battle of Kosovo">Battle of Kosovo</a>, <a href="/wiki/History_of_Ottoman_Kosovo" class="mw-redirect" title="History of Ottoman Kosovo">History of Ottoman Kosovo</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Vilayet_of_Kosovo" class="mw-redirect" title="Vilayet of Kosovo">Vilayet of Kosovo</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Xhamia_e_Madhe_Prishtine.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4d/Xhamia_e_Madhe_Prishtine.JPG/220px-Xhamia_e_Madhe_Prishtine.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="330" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4d/Xhamia_e_Madhe_Prishtine.JPG/330px-Xhamia_e_Madhe_Prishtine.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4d/Xhamia_e_Madhe_Prishtine.JPG/440px-Xhamia_e_Madhe_Prishtine.JPG 2x" data-file-width="4000" data-file-height="6000" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/Imperial_Mosque_(Pristina)" title="Imperial Mosque (Pristina)">Imperial Mosque of Pristina</a> built by <a href="/wiki/Mehmed_the_Conqueror" class="mw-redirect" title="Mehmed the Conqueror">Sultan Mehmed the Conqueror</a>, 1461</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1389, as the <a href="/wiki/Ottoman_Empire" title="Ottoman Empire">Ottoman Empire</a> expanded northwards through the Balkans, Ottoman forces under Sultan <a href="/wiki/Murad_I" title="Murad I">Murad I</a> met with a Christian coalition led by <a href="/wiki/Moravian_Serbia" title="Moravian Serbia">Moravian Serbia</a> under <a href="/wiki/Lazar_of_Serbia" title="Lazar of Serbia">Prince Lazar</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Kosovo" title="Battle of Kosovo">Battle of Kosovo</a>. Both sides suffered heavy losses and the battle was a stalemate and it was even reported as a Christian victory at first, but Serbian manpower was depleted and <i>de facto</i> Serbian rulers couldn't raise another equal force to the Ottoman army.<sup id="cite_ref-Jelavich1983_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Jelavich1983-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-prospect-magazine.co.uk_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-prospect-magazine.co.uk-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> Different parts of Kosovo were ruled directly or indirectly by the Ottomans in this early period. The medieval town of <a href="/wiki/Novo_Brdo" title="Novo Brdo">Novo Brdo</a> was under Lazar's son, <a href="/wiki/Stefan_Lazarevi%C4%87" title="Stefan Lazarević">Stefan</a> who became a loyal Ottoman vassal and instigated the downfall of <a href="/wiki/Vuk_Brankovi%C4%87" title="Vuk Branković">Vuk Branković</a> who eventually joined the Hungarian anti-Ottoman coalition and was defeated in 1395–96. A small part of Vuk's land with the villages of Priština and Vučitrn was given to his sons to hold as Ottoman vassals for a brief period.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> By 1455-57, the Ottoman Empire assumed direct control of all of Kosovo and the region remained part of the empire until 1912. During this period, <a href="/wiki/Islam" title="Islam">Islam</a> was introduced to the region.
</p><p>Although initially stout opponents of the advancing Turks, Albanian chiefs ultimately came to accept the Ottomans as sovereigns. The resulting alliance facilitated the mass conversion of Albanians to Islam. Given that the Ottoman Empire's subjects were divided along religious (rather than ethnic) lines, the spread of Islam greatly elevated the status of Albanian chiefs. Centuries earlier, Albanians of Kosovo were predominantly Christian and Albanians and Serbs for the most part co-existed peacefully. The Ottomans appeared to have a more deliberate approach to converting the Roman Catholic population who were mostly Albanians in comparison with the mostly Serbian adherents of Eastern Orthodoxy, as they viewed the former less favorably due to its allegiance to Rome, a competing regional power.<sup id="cite_ref-Cohen_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Cohen-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the 19th century, there was an <a href="/wiki/Rise_of_nationalism_under_the_Ottoman_Empire" class="mw-redirect" title="Rise of nationalism under the Ottoman Empire">awakening</a> of <a href="/wiki/Ethnic_nationalism" title="Ethnic nationalism">ethnic nationalism</a> throughout the Balkans. The underlying ethnic tensions became part of a broader struggle of Christian Serbs against Muslim Albanians.<sup id="cite_ref-prospect-magazine.co.uk_44-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-prospect-magazine.co.uk-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> The ethnic <a href="/wiki/National_Renaissance_of_Albania" class="mw-redirect" title="National Renaissance of Albania">Albanian nationalism</a> movement was centred in Kosovo. In 1878 the <a href="/wiki/League_of_Prizren" title="League of Prizren">League of Prizren</a> (<span title="Albanian-language text"><i lang="sq">Lidhja e Prizrenit</i></span>) was formed, a political organisation that sought to unify all the Albanians of the Ottoman Empire in a common struggle for autonomy and greater cultural rights,<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> although they generally desired the continuation of the Ottoman Empire.<sup id="cite_ref-Cirkovic._p._244_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Cirkovic._p._244-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> The League was dis-established in 1881 but enabled the awakening of a <a href="/wiki/Nation" title="Nation">national identity</a> among Albanians,<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> whose ambitions competed with those of the Serbs, the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Serbia" title="Kingdom of Serbia">Kingdom of Serbia</a> wishing to incorporate this land that had formerly been within its empire.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Qendra_historike_e_Prizrenitaa.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/Qendra_historike_e_Prizrenitaa.jpg/220px-Qendra_historike_e_Prizrenitaa.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="155" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/Qendra_historike_e_Prizrenitaa.jpg/330px-Qendra_historike_e_Prizrenitaa.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/Qendra_historike_e_Prizrenitaa.jpg/440px-Qendra_historike_e_Prizrenitaa.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4528" data-file-height="3200" /></a><figcaption>The city of <a href="/wiki/Prizren" title="Prizren">Prizren</a> was the cultural and intellectual centre of Kosovo during the Ottoman period in the Middle Ages and is now the historic capital of Kosovo.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The modern Albanian-Serbian conflict has its roots in the <a href="/wiki/Expulsion_of_the_Albanians_1877%E2%80%931878" class="mw-redirect" title="Expulsion of the Albanians 1877–1878">expulsion of the Albanians in 1877–1878</a> from areas that became incorporated into the <a href="/wiki/Principality_of_Serbia" title="Principality of Serbia">Principality of Serbia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> During and after the <a href="/wiki/Serbian%E2%80%93Ottoman_War_(1876%E2%80%9378)" class="mw-redirect" title="Serbian–Ottoman War (1876–78)">Serbian–Ottoman War of 1876–78</a>, between 30,000 and 70,000 Muslims, mostly Albanians, were expelled by the <a href="/wiki/Armed_forces_of_the_Principality_of_Serbia" class="mw-redirect" title="Armed forces of the Principality of Serbia">Serb army</a> from the <a href="/wiki/Sanjak_of_Ni%C5%A1" title="Sanjak of Niš">Sanjak of Niš</a> and fled to the <a href="/wiki/Kosovo_Vilayet" class="mw-redirect" title="Kosovo Vilayet">Kosovo Vilayet</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Batakovic1992_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Batakovic1992-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Elsie2010_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Elsie2010-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> According to Austrian data, by the 1890s Kosovo was 70% Muslim (nearly entirely of Albanian descent) and less than 30% non-Muslim (primarily Serbs).<sup id="cite_ref-Cohen_46-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Cohen-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> In May 1901, Albanians pillaged and partially burned the cities of Novi Pazar, Sjenica and Pristina, and <a href="/wiki/1901_massacres_of_Serbs" title="1901 massacres of Serbs">massacred Serbs</a> in the area of Kolašin.<sup id="cite_ref-King-Mason-30_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-King-Mason-30-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Kingdom_of_Yugoslavia">Kingdom of Yugoslavia</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Serbia1913.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/58/Serbia1913.png/220px-Serbia1913.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="303" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/58/Serbia1913.png/330px-Serbia1913.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/58/Serbia1913.png/440px-Serbia1913.png 2x" data-file-width="663" data-file-height="912" /></a><figcaption>Division of <a href="/wiki/Kosovo_vilayet" title="Kosovo vilayet">Kosovo vilayet</a> between the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Serbia" title="Kingdom of Serbia">Kingdom of Serbia</a> (<i>yellow</i>) and the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Montenegro" title="Kingdom of Montenegro">Kingdom of Montenegro</a> (<i>green</i>) following the <a href="/wiki/Balkan_Wars" title="Balkan Wars">Balkan Wars</a> 1913.</figcaption></figure>
<p>In the spring of 1912, Albanians under the lead of <a href="/wiki/Hasan_Prishtina" title="Hasan Prishtina">Hasan Prishtina</a> <a href="/wiki/Albanian_revolt_of_1912" title="Albanian revolt of 1912">revolted</a> against the Ottoman Empire. The rebels were joined by a wave of Albanians in the <a href="/wiki/Military_of_the_Ottoman_Empire" title="Military of the Ottoman Empire">Ottoman army</a> ranks, who deserted the army, refusing to fight their own kin. The rebels defeated the Ottomans and the latter were forced to accept all fourteen demands of the rebels, which foresaw an effective autonomy for the Albanians living in the Empire.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMalcolm1998246-248_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMalcolm1998246-248-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> However, this autonomy never materialized, and the revolt created serious weaknesses in the Ottoman ranks, luring <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Montenegro" title="Kingdom of Montenegro">Montenegro</a>, <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Serbia" title="Kingdom of Serbia">Serbia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Bulgaria" title="Kingdom of Bulgaria">Bulgaria</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Greece" title="Kingdom of Greece">Greece</a> into declaring war on the Ottoman Empire and starting the <a href="/wiki/First_Balkan_War" title="First Balkan War">First Balkan War</a>. 
</p><p>After the Ottomans' defeat in the <a href="/wiki/First_Balkan_War" title="First Balkan War">First Balkan War</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_London_(1913)" title="Treaty of London (1913)">1913 Treaty of London</a> was signed with Metohija ceded to the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Montenegro" title="Kingdom of Montenegro">Kingdom of Montenegro</a> and eastern Kosovo ceded to the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Serbia" title="Kingdom of Serbia">Kingdom of Serbia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> During the <a href="/wiki/Balkan_Wars" title="Balkan Wars">Balkan Wars</a>, over 100,000 Albanians left Kosovo and about 50,000 were killed in the <a href="/wiki/Massacres_of_Albanians_in_the_Balkan_Wars" title="Massacres of Albanians in the Balkan Wars">massacres</a> that accompanied the war.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> Soon, there were concerted <a href="/wiki/Yugoslav_colonization_of_Kosovo" title="Yugoslav colonization of Kosovo">Serbian colonisation efforts</a> in Kosovo during various periods between Serbia's 1912 takeover of the province and <a href="/wiki/World_War_II" title="World War II">World War&#160;II</a>, causing the population of Serbs in Kosovo to grow by about 58,000 in this period.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMalcolm1998279_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMalcolm1998279-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Serbian authorities promoted creating new Serb settlements in Kosovo as well as the assimilation of Albanians into Serbian society, causing a mass exodus of Albanians from Kosovo.<sup id="cite_ref-Schabnel_2001_20_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Schabnel_2001_20-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> The figures of Albanians forcefully expelled from Kosovo range between 60,000 and 239,807, while Malcolm mentions 100,000–120,000. In combination with the politics of extermination and expulsion, there was also a process of assimilation through religious conversion of Albanian Muslims and Albanian Catholics into the Serbian Orthodox religion which took place as early as 1912. These politics seem to have been inspired by the nationalist ideologies of <a href="/wiki/Ilija_Gara%C5%A1anin" title="Ilija Garašanin">Ilija Garašanin</a> and <a href="/wiki/Jovan_Cviji%C4%87" title="Jovan Cvijić">Jovan Cvijić</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:NY_Times_Massacre_of_Albanians_1912.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6d/NY_Times_Massacre_of_Albanians_1912.jpg/220px-NY_Times_Massacre_of_Albanians_1912.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="235" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6d/NY_Times_Massacre_of_Albanians_1912.jpg/330px-NY_Times_Massacre_of_Albanians_1912.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6d/NY_Times_Massacre_of_Albanians_1912.jpg/440px-NY_Times_Massacre_of_Albanians_1912.jpg 2x" data-file-width="622" data-file-height="665" /></a><figcaption>31 December 1912 <i><a href="/wiki/The_New_York_Times" title="The New York Times">New York Times</a></i> headline regarding the <a href="/wiki/Massacres_of_Albanians_in_the_Balkan_Wars" title="Massacres of Albanians in the Balkan Wars">Massacres of Albanians in the Balkan Wars</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>In the winter of 1915–16, during <a href="/wiki/World_War_I" title="World War I">World War I</a>, Kosovo saw the retreat of the Serbian army as Kosovo was occupied by <a href="/wiki/Bulgaria" title="Bulgaria">Bulgaria</a> and <a href="/wiki/Austria-Hungary" title="Austria-Hungary">Austria-Hungary</a>. In 1918, the <a href="/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_I" title="Allies of World War I">Allied Powers</a> pushed the <a href="/wiki/Central_Powers" title="Central Powers">Central Powers</a> out of Kosovo. 
</p><p>A new administration system since 26 April 1922 split Kosovo among three districts (<a href="/wiki/Oblast" title="Oblast">oblast</a>) of the Kingdom: Kosovo, Raška and Zeta. In 1929, the country was transformed into the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Yugoslavia" title="Kingdom of Yugoslavia">Kingdom of Yugoslavia</a> and the territories of Kosovo were reorganised among the <a href="/wiki/Zeta_Banovina" title="Zeta Banovina">Banate of Zeta</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Morava_Banovina" title="Morava Banovina">Banate of Morava</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Vardar_Banovina" title="Vardar Banovina">Banate of Vardar</a>. In order to change the <a href="/wiki/Demographics_of_Kosovo" title="Demographics of Kosovo">ethnic composition of Kosovo</a>, between 1912 and 1941 a <a href="/wiki/Colonisation_of_Kosovo" class="mw-redirect" title="Colonisation of Kosovo">large-scale Serbian colonisation of Kosovo</a> was undertaken by the Belgrade government. Kosovar Albanians' right to receive education in their own language <a href="/wiki/Persecution_of_Albanians_in_the_Kingdom_of_Yugoslavia" class="mw-redirect" title="Persecution of Albanians in the Kingdom of Yugoslavia">was denied</a> alongside other non-Slavic or unrecognised Slavic nations of Yugoslavia, as the kingdom only recognised the Slavic Croat, Serb, and Slovene nations as constituent nations of Yugoslavia. Other Slavs had to identify as one of the three official Slavic nations and non-Slav nations deemed as minorities.<sup id="cite_ref-Schabnel_2001_20_66-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Schabnel_2001_20-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Photograph_showing_Serbian_soldiers_posing_with_5_executed_Albanians_in_Prizren_in_1925.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0a/Photograph_showing_Serbian_soldiers_posing_with_5_executed_Albanians_in_Prizren_in_1925.jpg/220px-Photograph_showing_Serbian_soldiers_posing_with_5_executed_Albanians_in_Prizren_in_1925.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="136" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0a/Photograph_showing_Serbian_soldiers_posing_with_5_executed_Albanians_in_Prizren_in_1925.jpg/330px-Photograph_showing_Serbian_soldiers_posing_with_5_executed_Albanians_in_Prizren_in_1925.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0a/Photograph_showing_Serbian_soldiers_posing_with_5_executed_Albanians_in_Prizren_in_1925.jpg/440px-Photograph_showing_Serbian_soldiers_posing_with_5_executed_Albanians_in_Prizren_in_1925.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1073" data-file-height="663" /></a><figcaption>Photograph showing <a href="/wiki/Serbian_army" class="mw-redirect" title="Serbian army">Serbian soldiers</a> posing with 5 executed <a href="/wiki/Albanians" title="Albanians">Albanians</a> in <a href="/wiki/Prizren" title="Prizren">Prizren</a> in <a href="/wiki/1925" title="1925">1925</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Albanians and other <a href="/wiki/Muslims" title="Muslims">Muslims</a> were forced to emigrate, mainly with the land reform which struck Albanian landowners in 1919, but also with direct violent measures.<sup id="cite_ref-daskalovski_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-daskalovski-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMalcolm1998_69-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMalcolm1998-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> In 1935 and 1938, two agreements between the Kingdom of Yugoslavia and Turkey were signed on the expatriation of 240,000 Albanians to Turkey, but the expatriation did not occur due to the outbreak of <a href="/wiki/World_War_II" title="World War II">World War II</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Germans_in_Kosovska_Mitrovica.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/27/Germans_in_Kosovska_Mitrovica.jpg/190px-Germans_in_Kosovska_Mitrovica.jpg" decoding="async" width="190" height="257" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/27/Germans_in_Kosovska_Mitrovica.jpg/285px-Germans_in_Kosovska_Mitrovica.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/27/Germans_in_Kosovska_Mitrovica.jpg/380px-Germans_in_Kosovska_Mitrovica.jpg 2x" data-file-width="776" data-file-height="1050" /></a><figcaption>German soldiers set fire to a Serbian village near <a href="/wiki/Mitrovica,_Kosovo" title="Mitrovica, Kosovo">Mitrovica</a>, circa 1941.</figcaption></figure>
<p>After the <a href="/wiki/Axis_invasion_of_Yugoslavia" class="mw-redirect" title="Axis invasion of Yugoslavia">Axis invasion of Yugoslavia</a> in 1941, most of Kosovo was assigned to Italian-controlled Albania, and the rest was controlled by Germany and Bulgaria. A three-dimensional conflict ensued, involving inter-ethnic, ideological, and international affiliations.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMalcolm1998312_71-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMalcolm1998312-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> Albanian collaborators persecuted Serb and Montenegrin settlers.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> Estimates differ, but most authors estimate that between 3,000 and 10,000 Serbs and Montenegrins died in Kosovo during the Second World War. Another 30,000 to 40,000 Serbs and Montenegrins, mainly settlers, were deported from Kosovo.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMalcolm1998312_71-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMalcolm1998312-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> A decree from Yugoslav leader <a href="/wiki/Josip_Broz_Tito" title="Josip Broz Tito">Josip Broz Tito</a>, followed by a new law in August 1945 disallowed the return of colonists who had taken land from Albanian peasants.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMalcolm1998317-318_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMalcolm1998317-318-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> During the war years, some Serbs and Montenegrins were sent to concentration camps in Pristina and Mitrovica.<sup id="cite_ref-Ramet2006_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ramet2006-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> Nonetheless, these conflicts were relatively low-level compared with other areas of Yugoslavia during the war years. Two Serb historians also estimate that 12,000 Albanians died.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMalcolm1998312_71-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMalcolm1998312-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> An official investigation conducted by the Yugoslav government in 1964 recorded nearly 8,000 war-related fatalities in Kosovo between 1941 and 1945, 5,489 of them Serb or Montenegrin and 2,177 Albanian.<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> Some sources note that up to 72,000 individuals were encouraged to settle or resettle into Kosovo from Albania by the short-lived Italian administration.<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Ramet2006_74-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ramet2006-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> As the regime collapsed, this was never materialized with historians and contemporary references emphasizing that a large-scale migration of Albanians from Albania to Kosovo is not recorded in Axis documents.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMalcolm1998312–313_77-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMalcolm1998312–313-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div style="clear:both;"></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Communist_Yugoslavia">Communist Yugoslavia</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Socialist_Autonomous_Province_of_Kosovo" title="Socialist Autonomous Province of Kosovo">Socialist Autonomous Province of Kosovo</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Flag_of_SFR_Yugoslav_Albanian_Minority.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/54/Flag_of_SFR_Yugoslav_Albanian_Minority.svg/220px-Flag_of_SFR_Yugoslav_Albanian_Minority.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="110" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/54/Flag_of_SFR_Yugoslav_Albanian_Minority.svg/330px-Flag_of_SFR_Yugoslav_Albanian_Minority.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/54/Flag_of_SFR_Yugoslav_Albanian_Minority.svg/440px-Flag_of_SFR_Yugoslav_Albanian_Minority.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1000" data-file-height="500" /></a><figcaption>The flag of the Albanian minority of Kosovo in the Socialist Federal Republic of Yugoslavia.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The province in its current form first took shape in 1945 as the <i>Autonomous Region of Kosovo and Metohija</i>, with a final demarcation in 1959.<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> Until 1945, the only entity bearing the name of Kosovo in the late modern period had been the Vilayet of Kosovo, a political unit created by the Ottoman Empire in 1877. However, its borders were dissimilar to the current borders.<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>Tensions between ethnic Albanians and the Yugoslav government were significant, not only due to ethnic tensions but also due to political ideological concerns, especially regarding relations with neighbouring Albania.<sup id="cite_ref-Kosovo._2000._Pp._35_81-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kosovo._2000._Pp._35-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> Harsh repressive measures were imposed on Kosovo Albanians due to suspicions that there were sympathisers of the <a href="/wiki/Stalinism" title="Stalinism">Stalinist</a> regime of <a href="/wiki/Enver_Hoxha" title="Enver Hoxha">Enver Hoxha</a> of Albania.<sup id="cite_ref-Kosovo._2000._Pp._35_81-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kosovo._2000._Pp._35-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> In 1956, a show trial in Pristina was held in which multiple Albanian Communists of Kosovo were convicted of being infiltrators from Albania and given long prison sentences.<sup id="cite_ref-Kosovo._2000._Pp._35_81-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kosovo._2000._Pp._35-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> High-ranking Serbian communist official <a href="/wiki/Aleksandar_Rankovi%C4%87" title="Aleksandar Ranković">Aleksandar Ranković</a> sought to secure the position of the Serbs in Kosovo and gave them dominance in Kosovo's <a href="/wiki/Nomenklatura" title="Nomenklatura">nomenklatura</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Fadil_Hoxha,_commander_of_Kosovo_partisans.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e3/Fadil_Hoxha%2C_commander_of_Kosovo_partisans.jpg/170px-Fadil_Hoxha%2C_commander_of_Kosovo_partisans.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="225" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e3/Fadil_Hoxha%2C_commander_of_Kosovo_partisans.jpg/255px-Fadil_Hoxha%2C_commander_of_Kosovo_partisans.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e3/Fadil_Hoxha%2C_commander_of_Kosovo_partisans.jpg/340px-Fadil_Hoxha%2C_commander_of_Kosovo_partisans.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1362" data-file-height="1802" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Fadil_Hoxha" title="Fadil Hoxha">Fadil Hoxha</a>, the vice-president of <a href="/wiki/Socialist_Federal_Republic_of_Yugoslavia" title="Socialist Federal Republic of Yugoslavia">Socialist Federal Republic of Yugoslavia</a>, from 1978 to 1979.</figcaption></figure>
<p><a href="/wiki/Islam" title="Islam">Islam</a> in Kosovo at this time was repressed and both Albanians and Muslim Slavs were encouraged to declare themselves to be Turkish and emigrate to Turkey.<sup id="cite_ref-Kosovo._2000._Pp._35_81-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kosovo._2000._Pp._35-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> At the same time Serbs and Montenegrins dominated the government, security forces, and industrial employment in Kosovo.<sup id="cite_ref-Kosovo._2000._Pp._35_81-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kosovo._2000._Pp._35-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> Albanians resented these conditions and protested against them in the late 1960s, caling the actions taken by authorities in Kosovo colonialist, and demanding that Kosovo be made a republic, or declaring support for Albania.<sup id="cite_ref-Kosovo._2000._Pp._35_81-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kosovo._2000._Pp._35-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After the ouster of Ranković in 1966, the agenda of pro-decentralisation reformers in Yugoslavia succeeded in the late 1960s in attaining substantial decentralisation of powers, creating substantial autonomy in Kosovo and Vojvodina, and recognising a Muslim Yugoslav nationality.<sup id="cite_ref-Melissa_Katherine_Bokovoy_1992._Pp._296_83-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Melissa_Katherine_Bokovoy_1992._Pp._296-83">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> As a result of these reforms, there was a massive overhaul of Kosovo's nomenklatura and police, that shifted from being Serb-dominated to ethnic Albanian-dominated through firing Serbs in large scale.<sup id="cite_ref-Melissa_Katherine_Bokovoy_1992._Pp._296_83-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Melissa_Katherine_Bokovoy_1992._Pp._296-83">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> Further concessions were made to the ethnic Albanians of Kosovo in response to unrest, including the creation of the <a href="/wiki/Universiteti_i_Prishtin%C3%ABs" class="mw-redirect" title="Universiteti i Prishtinës">University of Pristina</a> as an <a href="/wiki/Albanian_language" title="Albanian language">Albanian language</a> institution.<sup id="cite_ref-Melissa_Katherine_Bokovoy_1992._Pp._296_83-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Melissa_Katherine_Bokovoy_1992._Pp._296-83">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> These changes created widespread fear among Serbs that they were being made <a href="/wiki/Second-class_citizen" title="Second-class citizen">second-class citizens</a> in Yugoslavia.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> By the 1974 Constitution of Yugoslavia, Kosovo was granted major autonomy, allowing it to have its own administration, assembly, and judiciary; as well as having a membership in the collective presidency and the Yugoslav parliament, in which it held veto power.<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:SocialistYugoslavia_en.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/77/SocialistYugoslavia_en.svg/290px-SocialistYugoslavia_en.svg.png" decoding="async" width="290" height="210" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/77/SocialistYugoslavia_en.svg/435px-SocialistYugoslavia_en.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/77/SocialistYugoslavia_en.svg/580px-SocialistYugoslavia_en.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="595" data-file-height="431" /></a><figcaption>Republics and provinces of the SFR Yugoslavia.</figcaption></figure>
<p>In the aftermath of the 1974 constitution, concerns over the rise of Albanian nationalism in Kosovo rose with the widespread celebrations in 1978 of the 100th anniversary of the founding of the <a href="/wiki/League_of_Prizren" title="League of Prizren">League of Prizren</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Kosovo._2000._Pp._35_81-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kosovo._2000._Pp._35-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> Albanians felt that their status as a "minority" in Yugoslavia had made them second-class citizens in comparison with the "nations" of Yugoslavia and demanded that Kosovo be a <a href="/wiki/Constituent_state" class="mw-redirect" title="Constituent state">constituent republic</a>, alongside the other republics of Yugoslavia.<sup id="cite_ref-Kosovo._2000._Pp._36_86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kosovo._2000._Pp._36-86">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/1981_protests_in_Kosovo" title="1981 protests in Kosovo">Protests by Albanians in 1981 over the status of Kosovo</a> resulted in Yugoslav territorial defence units being brought into Kosovo and a state of emergency being declared resulting in violence and the protests being crushed.<sup id="cite_ref-Kosovo._2000._Pp._36_86-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kosovo._2000._Pp._36-86">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> In the aftermath of the 1981 protests, purges took place in the Communist Party, and rights that had been recently granted to Albanians were rescinded&#160;– including ending the provision of Albanian professors and Albanian language textbooks in the education system.<sup id="cite_ref-Kosovo._2000._Pp._36_86-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kosovo._2000._Pp._36-86">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>While Albanians in the region had the highest birth rates in Europe, other areas of Yugoslavia including Serbia had low birth rates. Increased urbanization and economic development led to higher settlements of Albanian workers into Serb-majority areas, as Serbs departed in response to the economic climate for more favorable real estate conditions in Serbia.<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> While there was tension, charges of "genocide" and planned harassment have been discredited as a pretext to revoke Kosovo's autonomy. For example, in 1986 the Serbian Orthodox Church published an official claim that Kosovo Serbs were being subjected to an Albanian program of 'genocide'.<sup id="cite_ref-books.google.com_88-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-books.google.com-88">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Even though they were disproved by police statistics,<sup id="cite_ref-books.google.com_88-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-books.google.com-88">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources"><span title="This citation requires a reference to the specific page or range of pages in which the material appears. (June 2014)">page&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> they received wide attention in the Serbian press and that led to further ethnic problems and eventual removal of Kosovo's status. Beginning in March 1981, Kosovar Albanian students of the University of Pristina organised protests seeking that Kosovo become a republic within Yugoslavia and demanding their human rights.<sup id="cite_ref-nyt19810419_89-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nyt19810419-89">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> The protests were brutally suppressed by the police and army, with many protesters arrested.<sup id="cite_ref-hdk_90-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-hdk-90">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> During the 1980s, ethnic tensions continued with frequent violent outbreaks against Yugoslav state authorities, resulting in a further increase in emigration of Kosovo Serbs and other ethnic groups.<sup id="cite_ref-reuters19860527_91-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-reuters19860527-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-csm19860728_92-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-csm19860728-92">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> The Yugoslav leadership tried to suppress protests of Kosovo Serbs seeking protection from ethnic discrimination and violence.<sup id="cite_ref-nyt19870627_93-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nyt19870627-93">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div style="clear:both;"></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Kosovo_War">Kosovo War</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Kosovo_War" title="Kosovo War">Kosovo War</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/War_crimes_in_the_Kosovo_War" title="War crimes in the Kosovo War">War crimes in the Kosovo War</a></div>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Dr._Ibrahim_Rugova.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d4/Dr._Ibrahim_Rugova.jpg/180px-Dr._Ibrahim_Rugova.jpg" decoding="async" width="180" height="206" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d4/Dr._Ibrahim_Rugova.jpg/270px-Dr._Ibrahim_Rugova.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d4/Dr._Ibrahim_Rugova.jpg/360px-Dr._Ibrahim_Rugova.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1200" data-file-height="1375" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Ibrahim_Rugova" title="Ibrahim Rugova">Ibrahim Rugova</a> played a significant role in advocating for the rights of Kosovo Albanians and their aspirations for self-determination.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Inter-ethnic tensions continued to worsen in Kosovo throughout the 1980s. In 1989, Serbian President <a href="/wiki/Slobodan_Milo%C5%A1evi%C4%87" title="Slobodan Milošević">Slobodan Milošević</a>, employing a mix of intimidation and political maneuvering, drastically reduced Kosovo's special autonomous status within Serbia and started cultural oppression of the ethnic Albanian population.<sup id="cite_ref-rogel_94-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-rogel-94">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> Kosovar Albanians responded with a <a href="/wiki/Non-violent_resistance" class="mw-redirect" title="Non-violent resistance">non-violent</a> separatist movement, employing widespread <a href="/wiki/Civil_disobedience" title="Civil disobedience">civil disobedience</a> and creation of parallel structures in <a href="/wiki/Medical_school" title="Medical school">education, medical</a> care, and taxation, with the ultimate goal of achieving the <a href="/wiki/Independence_of_Kosovo" class="mw-redirect" title="Independence of Kosovo">independence of Kosovo</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In July 1990, the Kosovo Albanians proclaimed the existence of the <a href="/wiki/Republic_of_Kosova_(1990%E2%80%932000)" class="mw-redirect" title="Republic of Kosova (1990–2000)">Republic of Kosova</a>, and declared it a sovereign and independent state in September 1992.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMalcolm1998346–347_96-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMalcolm1998346–347-96">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> In May 1992, <a href="/wiki/Ibrahim_Rugova" title="Ibrahim Rugova">Ibrahim Rugova</a> was elected its president in an election in which only Kosovo Albanians participated.<sup id="cite_ref-babuna_97-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-babuna-97">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> During its lifetime, the Republic of Kosova was only officially <a href="/wiki/Diplomatic_recognition" title="Diplomatic recognition">recognised</a> by Albania. By the mid-1990s, the Kosovo Albanian population was growing restless, as the status of Kosovo was not resolved as part of the <a href="/wiki/Dayton_Agreement" title="Dayton Agreement">Dayton Agreement</a> of November 1995, which ended the <a href="/wiki/Bosnian_War" title="Bosnian War">Bosnian War</a>. By 1996, the <a href="/wiki/Kosovo_Liberation_Army" title="Kosovo Liberation Army">Kosovo Liberation Army</a> (KLA), an ethnic Albanian guerrilla <a href="/wiki/Paramilitary" title="Paramilitary">paramilitary group</a> that sought the separation of Kosovo and the eventual creation of a <a href="/wiki/Greater_Albania" title="Greater Albania">Greater Albania</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> had prevailed over the Rugova's non-violent resistance movement and launched attacks against the Yugoslav Army and Serbian police in Kosovo, resulting in the <a href="/wiki/Kosovo_War" title="Kosovo War">Kosovo War</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-rogel_94-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-rogel-94">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>By 1998, international pressure compelled Yugoslavia to sign a ceasefire and partially withdraw its security forces. Events were to be monitored by <a href="/wiki/Organization_for_Security_and_Co-operation_in_Europe" title="Organization for Security and Co-operation in Europe">Organization for Security and Co-operation in Europe</a> (OSCE) observers according to an agreement negotiated by <a href="/wiki/Richard_Holbrooke" title="Richard Holbrooke">Richard Holbrooke</a>. The ceasefire did not hold and fighting resumed in December 1998, culminating in the <a href="/wiki/Ra%C4%8Dak_massacre" title="Račak massacre">Račak massacre</a>, which attracted further international attention to the conflict.<sup id="cite_ref-rogel_94-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-rogel-94">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> Within weeks, a multilateral international conference was convened and by March had prepared a draft agreement known as the <a href="/wiki/Rambouillet_Accords" class="mw-redirect" title="Rambouillet Accords">Rambouillet Accords</a>, calling for the restoration of Kosovo's autonomy and the deployment of <a href="/wiki/NATO" title="NATO">NATO</a> <a href="/wiki/Peacekeeping" title="Peacekeeping">peacekeeping</a> forces. The Yugoslav delegation found the terms unacceptable and refused to sign the draft. Between 24 March and 10 June 1999, <a href="/wiki/1999_NATO_bombing_of_Yugoslavia" class="mw-redirect" title="1999 NATO bombing of Yugoslavia">NATO intervened</a> by bombing Yugoslavia, aiming to force Milošević to withdraw his forces from Kosovo,<sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> though NATO could not appeal to any particular motion of the <a href="/wiki/Security_Council_of_the_United_Nations" class="mw-redirect" title="Security Council of the United Nations">Security Council of the United Nations</a> to help legitimise its intervention. Combined with continued skirmishes between Albanian guerrillas and Yugoslav forces the conflict resulted in a further massive displacement of population in Kosovo.<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Missing_Men_of_Krusha_e_Madhe_(Burrat_e_Krushes_se_Madhe).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8c/Missing_Men_of_Krusha_e_Madhe_%28Burrat_e_Krushes_se_Madhe%29.jpg/220px-Missing_Men_of_Krusha_e_Madhe_%28Burrat_e_Krushes_se_Madhe%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="141" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8c/Missing_Men_of_Krusha_e_Madhe_%28Burrat_e_Krushes_se_Madhe%29.jpg/330px-Missing_Men_of_Krusha_e_Madhe_%28Burrat_e_Krushes_se_Madhe%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8c/Missing_Men_of_Krusha_e_Madhe_%28Burrat_e_Krushes_se_Madhe%29.jpg/440px-Missing_Men_of_Krusha_e_Madhe_%28Burrat_e_Krushes_se_Madhe%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="960" data-file-height="614" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Kosovo_Albanians" title="Kosovo Albanians">Kosovar Albanian</a> soldiers holding pictures in memory of the men who were killed or went missing in the <a href="/wiki/Krusha_massacres" title="Krusha massacres">Krusha massacres</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>During the conflict, roughly a million ethnic Albanians fled or were forcefully driven from Kosovo.
